### WFL-24

**A new version of a workflow leaves its waiting runs as they started** · must · functional · **implemented**

A changed workflow (a pack's new commit, a deploy) shall be imported in place under the same id, because n8n resumes a waiting execution from the copy of the workflow it keeps with that execution: a run waiting in the old version finishes on the old version, also when the node it waits on was renamed or removed, or the workflow unpublished or archived, and runs that start after the import run the new version. n8n's pruning shall never delete a waiting execution (it prunes finished ones only), so a run may wait for weeks.

- Parents: [SN-05](#sn-05)
- Sources: `docs/N8N-MIGRATION.md`, [DEC-51](#dec-51)
- Verified by: test: `tests/test_domain_packs.py::test_a_new_commit_replaces_its_workflows_in_place_and_a_refusal_unpublishes_them`, `tests/test_n8n_service.py::test_n8n_prunes_executions_after_30_days`
- Acceptance: Shown on a throwaway n8n 2.44.7 on 10 Nov 2026 (docs/N8N-MIGRATION.md §11): each waiting run resumed and succeeded on the version it started with; pruning at 1 hour and 1 execution deleted the finished runs and left the waiting ones, which then resumed. Test: a new pack commit replaces its workflows under the same ids.
- Trace: `ops/n8n_sync.py`, `docs/N8N-MIGRATION.md`

### WFL-25

**An agent step reads its tracker item** · should · functional · **implemented**

An agent step that names a tracker item (tracker and key) shall get, after its brief, the item as its tracker holds it when the step's task is made (its title, status and text, and every comment in order, the stack's own comments marked as its own and the others by their author, without the stack's write marks), its text kept from the start and its comments from the newest within the prompt's caps; an item that cannot be read shall fail the step with the reason, and a tracker the deployment does not have shall be refused before anything runs. The AI Stack step node shall offer the item for an agent step (Read the item of, Item).

- Parents: [SN-05](#sn-05)
- Sources: `docs/N8N-MIGRATION.md`, `docs/AI-STACK.md`
- Verified by: test: `tests/test_domain_trackers.py::test_an_agent_step_reads_its_item_with_the_stacks_own_comments_marked_as_its_own`, `tests/test_home_inbox.py::test_an_agent_step_whose_item_cannot_be_read_fails_with_the_reason_and_an_unknown_tracker_is_refused`, `tests/test_home_inbox.py::test_the_node_lets_an_agent_step_read_its_item`, `tests/test_home_inbox.py::test_an_issue_in_the_inbox_is_answered_waits_for_the_owners_reply_and_is_closed`
- Acceptance: Test: a stand-in item reads as its title, status, text and comments with the stack's own marked as yours and no write mark; an issue's agent steps get its title and text, then the whole conversation with the owner's reply; an item the forge does not have fails the step with the reason; an unknown tracker is refused; the node sends the item of an agent step.
- Trace: `ops/flows.py`, `ops/domain/trackers.py`, `n8n/custom/AistackStep.node.js`

### WFL-26

**The stack knows its own comments on one account** · must · functional · **implemented**

A comment on a tracker item or a watched pull request shall count as the stack's own when its author is the tracker's account for the stack (me) or when its last line is the stack's write mark, which every write of the stack ends with; this shall decide which comments a poll hands on, and so which events wake a waiting run, for every kind of tracker (Forgejo, Jira, the stand-in) and for pull requests. Where the stack writes with the owner's own account (home's Forgejo), the owner's reply, also one that quotes the stack's comment, shall wake the run, and the stack's own comment shall not.

- Parents: [SN-05](#sn-05)
- Sources: `docs/N8N-MIGRATION.md`
- Verified by: test: `tests/test_domain_trackers.py::test_the_stacks_own_comment_is_known_by_its_write_mark_where_it_writes_with_the_owners_account`, `tests/test_domain_trackers.py::test_jira_and_pull_request_comments_follow_the_same_rule_on_one_account`, `tests/test_home_inbox.py::test_an_issue_in_the_inbox_is_answered_waits_for_the_owners_reply_and_is_closed`
- Acceptance: Test: on one account the stack's marked answer and summary are its own and the owner's replies (plain, quoting it, ending in the quoted mark) are handed on; a Jira item and a pull request with one author hand on only the unmarked comment; end to end on home's inbox the stack's comment by the owner's account is recorded and the owner's unmarked reply wakes the run.
- Trace: `ops/domain/trackers.py`, `ops/domain/forgework.py`, `ops/runreg.py`

## Decisions

| Decision | Date | What | Supersedes |
|---|---|---|---|
| <a id="dec-01"></a>**DEC-01** Subscription only | 2026-10-27 | No paid model in the gateway, no fallback to one; all agent keys get subscription models only. Runs that hit the usage limit wait and retry. | the earlier $5 Anthropic + $5 OpenRouter development budget with per-key daily caps |
| <a id="dec-02"></a>**DEC-02** Everything in English | 2026-10-27 | Chat, app, reports, wikis and Telegram in English, reversing the earlier choice of German briefings. | - |
| <a id="dec-03"></a>**DEC-03** Builder in a sandbox with tap-to-deploy | 2026-10-27 | The builder codes in a sandbox, cannot push or deploy, and every change ships only after the owner's tap, with smoke test and automatic rollback (not full PC rights). | - |
| <a id="dec-04"></a>**DEC-04** Build order for hands in the world | 2026-10-28 | AI review and Semgrep, then Serena, then the share target and the old phone as a sealed AI phone, then browser hands and shopping missions that never buy on their own, then (later) sessions on the main phone. | - |
| <a id="dec-05"></a>**DEC-05** Observability first | 2026-10-28 | Every agent feature ships with its timeline, files read and changed, payoff, statistics and issue signals, clickable in Mission Control; a feature without it is unfinished. | - |
| <a id="dec-06"></a>**DEC-06** The guide is part of the Stack page | 2026-10-28 | The in-app guide lives on the Stack page, not as its own tab. | - |
| <a id="dec-07"></a>**DEC-07** Home Assistant on the NAS | 2026-10-29 | Home Assistant runs as a new container on the NAS (LAN only); nothing changes about existing NAS data or shares. One Home Assistant Voice speaker may be proposed for the owner to buy. | - |
| <a id="dec-08"></a>**DEC-08** The AI phone screen sleeps | 2026-10-29 | The AI phone's screen is no longer kept on (it drained the battery faster than it charged); it sleeps after 30 s and every action wakes it first. | 2026-10-28: the screen stays awake while connected (30 s wake loop, Stay awake) |
| <a id="dec-09"></a>**DEC-09** Needs you is only the owner's decisions | 2026-10-29 | Health alerts and the stack's own improvement ideas leave "Needs you" and show as "Stack upkeep". | - |
| <a id="dec-10"></a>**DEC-10** No caps, a lifecycle instead | 2026-10-29 | Capping how many ideas an agent may keep open is not the right approach; agents should reopen and refine ideas over many runs within a generic artifact lifecycle. | - |
| <a id="dec-11"></a>**DEC-11** Off-site key copy in OneDrive | 2026-10-25 | The age key is copied to OneDrive (own folder), not to a USB stick; Windows 11 upgrade declined. | - |
| <a id="dec-12"></a>**DEC-12** No ChatGPT-web bridge | 2026-10-27 | No browser automation of the ChatGPT web app as an API (terms of service, bot-detection evasion); not to be proposed again. | - |
| <a id="dec-13"></a>**DEC-13** KeepADB on the AI phone | 2026-10-29 | KeepADB 1.8.38 (F-Droid, checksum verified) may be installed so the AI phone starts in AI mode after a restart; each phone setting it needs runs only on the owner's Allow. | - |
| <a id="dec-14"></a>**DEC-14** Requirements engineering for the repo | 2026-10-29 | The stack's requirements are engineered retroactively, prepared for the agent swarm, kept current with every change, and contradictions between the parts are looked for and reported. | - |
| <a id="dec-15"></a>**DEC-15** Agent swarm design | 2026-10-29 | Agents on different harnesses and models get a shared artifact ledger with a generic lifecycle, a communication board (direct, multicast to all, rooms) and a chatroom, reusing existing technology. | - |
| <a id="dec-16"></a>**DEC-16** Claude Code as second engine | 2026-10-28 | Claude Code on the owner's Claude subscription becomes the builder's second engine, the default for code work when connected, and the reviewer of ChatGPT builds; code it reads is sent to Anthropic. | - |
| <a id="dec-17"></a>**DEC-17** The old phone is the AI phone | 2026-10-28 | The old Samsung AI phone (ai-phone) is the AI phone; the main phone (main phone) is not driven until a separate design for sessions on it. | - |
| <a id="dec-18"></a>**DEC-18** Voice through Alexa and a Voice speaker | 2026-10-29 | Talking to Hermes through the Echos ("Alexa, Hermes …") is wanted; a Home Assistant Voice speaker may follow. | 2026-10-28: the owner does not want voice |
| <a id="dec-19"></a>**DEC-19** Approve everything that ships | 2026-10-27 | The stack's own improvement proposals and builds ship only after the owner's approval. (Asked on 2026-10-29 whether small upkeep fixes may ship by themselves; no answer yet.) | - |
| <a id="dec-20"></a>**DEC-20** The owner does not run scripts | 2026-10-24 | Setup and recovery prefer routes that need no scripts or clicks from the owner; what only he can do (passwords, sign-ins, purchases, approvals) is asked for in one step with exact instructions. | - |
| <a id="dec-21"></a>**DEC-21** Swarm requirements before swarm code | 2026-10-29 | Before the agent swarm is built, its requirements are engineered to best practice and the current state of the art (A2A, MCP, the OWASP agentic top 10, multi-agent failure research), each with an acceptance criterion and a rollout phase. | - |
| <a id="dec-22"></a>**DEC-22** Switch over to the Ledger now | 2026-10-29 | No shadow week; the swarm's Ledger becomes the only store of the stack's ideas at once, the caps go, and the timelines, Claude Code traces and the session landing path are built in the same go ("Why wait for 5th? Implement all now and without gaps"). | the shadow week of SWM-87 |
| <a id="dec-23"></a>**DEC-23** The AI phone asks before typing or opening anything | 2026-10-29 | Typing text, opening a web address and opening an app on the AI phone always ask the owner, even inside a time window, so private data cannot leave through the phone unseen (answering FND-02 with the recommended option, "do all of this"). | - |
| <a id="dec-24"></a>**DEC-24** The swarm grows into parallel work, wider review and a live board | 2026-10-29 | After the phase 3 review (review 2026-10-29: full-scale swarm gaps (compared with Blitzy-style parallel code agents)), the owner asked for a full-scale, production-grade swarm. Phase 4 adds parallel work (a task graph, file leases, a merge queue, code-graph context) and independent reviewers; the Swarm page adds who is working right now and requests to the owner; phase 5 adds flow metrics. Governance stays: only the owner's taps decide, and every deploy needs the owner's approval. | - |
| <a id="dec-25"></a>**DEC-25** How phase 4 makes parallel work safe | 2026-10-30 | The owner asked to do phase 4 and close all gaps. The task graph lives in the Ledger (kind task, depends_on links), not in Hermes Kanban (SWM-21 deprecated): the Ledger already holds every artifact with its history and the owner's decisions, and the builders are not Hermes profiles. Two build slots on the 16 GB PC, one ChatGPT build at a time. Approved builds land through one merge queue, checked again on the combined code when main moved. The owner's assistant (main) reads and writes the Board but takes requests only from the owner (wake never), so no agent can steer the agent that holds the phone, mail and house tools. A reviewer's block stops the deploy; the owner may override it with a written reason (only his taps decide, DEC-24), and the override is evidence on the idea. | - |
| <a id="dec-26"></a>**DEC-26** How phase 5 lets the agents improve without rewriting themselves | 2026-10-30 | The owner asked to start phase 5. Agents are tuned only through agent changes: standing instructions on their card (or a model, tools or harness change), written by the Coach or the owner, never by the agent itself. A change is replayed on the agent's own past runs, with and without it, on the agent's own model, and is scored by code, not by a model judge: TF-IDF similarity to what the owner accepted, declined or merged (the Ledger's calibrated 0.20), and valid operations. It ships only when no score drops and its target rises; the owner still taps Build it and approves the build. After it ships it is watched for 14 days on real runs and one tap reverts it. Runs from before runs were recorded count as history examples (the evidence the agent cited, the artifact it wrote, its fate). Failures are classified into MAST by rules on the reason their label carries; what no rule matches stays unclassified. Requirements are mirrored into the Ledger (kind requirement), so ideas name the ones they serve. Phoenix keeps the replay sets and experiments as datasets for inspection; the Ledger keeps the record. Experiments use their own service key (vk-eval) so they never spend an agent's key. | - |
| <a id="dec-27"></a>**DEC-27** No truncation by default | 2026-10-30 | The owner, with a screenshot of cut log lines on the Swarm sync page: "I hate truncation. Do not truncate user facing input. Whenever you truncate anything I need a clear justification." Text the owner reads is shown whole; his own input is refused with the limit named, never cut; any remaining cap names its reason in the code (ops/truncation.py enforces it) and shows the cut when it hits. AI-written code tends to truncate by habit; the rules in AGENTS.md (Truncation) and the check exist so it cannot do so silently. | - |
| <a id="dec-28"></a>**DEC-28** Show the swarm as it works | 2026-10-30 | The owner found the Swarm page nearly unchanged after phases 4 and 5 and asked for timelines of when agents ran, interaction maps, the agents' conversations and a map of the agents; then "Actually I want all 4." The Board and the Ledger hold little (6 messages), while the runtime record holds every run and which run started which (a mission asked the scout 96 times in three days). So the four views are drawn from the runtime record first: Timeline, Map, the tool calls between agents in the Chatroom (marked as tool calls, not Board messages), and the team map in Agents. | - |
| <a id="dec-29"></a>**DEC-29** One list of ideas | 2026-10-30 | The review of 2026-10-30 found the stack's improvement ideas in three places: Code (The stack suggests: the crew's and the self-review's ideas), Agents (Improve: the Coach's agent changes) and Home (Stack upkeep, a count). The owner said "Yes, merge them". Both kinds are Ledger artifacts decided with the same Build it and Dismiss, so they are one ranked list under Agents → Ideas, each card marked with its kind; Code and Home only link to it. The numbers of the improvement loop (replay sets, failure classes, requirements in the Ledger) moved to Flow. | - |
| <a id="dec-30"></a>**DEC-30** The Chatroom is the team room, drawn from the agents' work | 2026-10-30 | The owner did not like the Chatroom (a list of rooms, most "no messages yet") and asked for a common place to read how the agents coordinate, with filters by agent and topic, without forcing agents to contribute, each agent responsible for its own lane first and stepping into another's only with something its owner would not see. Research (MetaGPT, Magentic-One, Anthropic's research system, Claude Code agent teams, the MAST failure study, stigmergy) and the stack's own numbers (6 Board messages ever, one cross-lane step, about 100 tool-call asks a day) say coordination works best through the shared work, with rare targeted messages. Part 1 (built): the Chatroom draws one timeline from the work with filters, and marks work on another agent's artifact as help. Part 2 (built, "Yes, build part 2"): a team roster and the lane rule at the start of every run, and tools to look into another agent's work (SWM-108). Part 3 (built, "Yes, build part 3": SWM-109) (lane discipline: a step into another lane names why its owner would not see it, a daily budget, and the owner agent's verdict feeding the scorecards) wait for the owner. | - |
| <a id="dec-31"></a>**DEC-31** The stack's memories as one system | 2026-10-30 | The owner asked for an overview of every memory system, a coherent design, the wikis' bubble map on the Wiki tab and where it is seen which agent uses which wiki, and asked whether one wiki should hold all the others. The review found ten stores (vault notes, vault wiki, agent wikis, journals, mission notes, Hindsight, the Ledger, skills, run records, the search index) with overlaps and no curator for three. Following CoALA (episodic, semantic, procedural), the LLM-wiki pattern (raw sources, one curator, ingest, query, lint) and scoped stores: no wiki that merges all the others (it would mix topics and owners), the vault wiki as the top layer with hub pages for shared subjects, one writer per wiki, reading scoped by the card, Hindsight for personal facts only, and every store with a limit, a curator and a view. Part 1 (built, "Yes, build part 1"): the inventory, the Memory section on Stack, the wikis' map and who reads and writes which wiki. Part 2 (built, "Yes, build part 2"): one opening per run with the swarm's pack inside, the working state in the journal, reading scoped by the card, one writer per wiki folding in helps, no journal copies in Hindsight (MEM-03 to MEM-06). Part 3 (built, "Yes, build part 3"): the Librarian (MEM-08, MEM-12, DEC-32). Part 4 (built, "Yes, build part 4"): prompts as files, tested (MEM-11, DEC-33). | - |
| <a id="dec-32"></a>**DEC-32** The Librarian tidies every wiki and adds nothing | 2026-10-30 | The owner said "Yes, build part 3". The memory curator, which already tidies the journals and long-term memory, becomes the Librarian, with no new agent or key: once a week it lints every agent wiki, marks pages not confirmed for 90 days as stale (agent wikis hold news; the vault wiki keeps its own 180-day rule), and asks one model call per wiki which candidate pairs are one subject and which claims contradict. It is the one change to a wiki besides its writer's (MEM-03), and it only rearranges: a merge keeps every link of both pages and leaves a stub that points on, a contradiction is a callout with both sources already on the page, and nothing is deleted except its own hub pages whose subject is no longer shared. Hubs are the top layer the owner asked about: one vault-wiki page per subject two or more wikis share, linking each wiki's page, instead of one wiki holding all the others. It also runs qmd cleanup. Each pass is one vault commit; a store over its limit for 8 days alerts its curator. | - |
| <a id="dec-33"></a>**DEC-33** Prompts are files, versioned by their text and changed only after a replay | 2026-10-30 | The owner said "Yes, build part 4". The 42 agent prompts of ops/ and the Hermes configure scripts moved verbatim into prompts/<owner>.md, one file per card (or service, or shared.md), each a marked section; the code loads them by name and shell scripts render them. A prompt's version is the sha of its text, so it can never be forgotten or bumped wrongly; each send records it, and the card hash follows the file. A change is accepted by a replay instead of a reviewer's taste: the prompt's last live cases (or committed cases), the approved and the new text on the agent's own model, a pairwise judge in random order, passing when the new text is valid as often and the judge prefers the old answer no more often. The verbatim move is each prompt's baseline, accepted only as its first record. The swarm's agent changes (SWM-18) keep their own scored replay of proposals. The desk's, the scout's and the privacy check's prompts followed ("Yes, do MEM-13"): their containers read prompts/ through a read-only mount (MEM-13). | - |
| <a id="dec-34"></a>**DEC-34** What the stack may do without asking | 2026-10-31 | The owner accepted the roadmap's proposed default ("Accept the default"). Without asking, the stack plans, staffs and runs tasks and may create temporary agents within read-only and sandboxed grants; it asks before it messages other people, spends money, deploys code, deletes data or acts on the AI phone. A task given in a sentence starts as soon as it is planned unless the owner chose "Show me the plan first"; every door keeps its own approvals (the deploy tap, purchases, the AI phone, Gmail's Trash). | - |
| <a id="dec-35"></a>**DEC-35** The plan graph lives with the tasks, not in the Ledger | 2026-10-31 | Building phase 2 ("Start phase 2"), the Conductor's plan graph and step journal went into the runner's task database (task_steps beside tasks, in the nightly backup) instead of the swarm's Ledger, as the roadmap first said. The Ledger's task model belongs to the idea-to-build flow (one plan per idea, builders claim its tasks) in a service capped at 256 MB, while a step has to be recorded in the same transaction that starts it, so a restart can never run a finished step again. The trace is the runtime record's (one run per task, a run per step inside it). A one-off research, analysis or errand task whose plan uses two or more agents goes to the Conductor; every other task keeps its door. | - |
| <a id="dec-36"></a>**DEC-36** Every planned task goes to the Conductor | 2026-10-31 | The owner asked for every task through the Conductor, recurring ones included ("Implement all properly", docs/SWARM-DESIGN.md). Since phase 6a every task the intake plans runs as a plan graph, one step or many, once or on a schedule, with a coding task's builder step as a build assignment and a code question as a Claude step; it replaces DEC-35's last sentence (only multi-agent one-off work went to the Conductor). Briefings, price watches and event-triggered tasks keep their mission until phase 6b, because the Topic Desk and the price watch are not yet steps. | - |
| <a id="dec-37"></a>**DEC-37** A price watch is its shopping agent's check; purchases stay the agent's runs | 2026-11-01 | The owner asked for price watches and event-triggered tasks under the Conductor ("Please do these two properly"). A price watch task holds its shopping agent (ops/shopping.py: limits, proposals, approvals, the order's tracking), made once with no schedule of its own; each occurrence of the task is one check of that agent (offers, a proposal or watching), and a waiting proposal that reaches its price or date starts the task's next occurrence. The purchase after the owner's tap and the order tracking stay the agent's own runs: they are the narrow deterministic path of docs/SHOPPING.md, with its own approval, and a plan step must not be able to start them. An event-triggered task runs each firing as an occurrence (its event in every step's prompt; events that come meanwhile wait, at most five); a briefing on an event is the desk's alert, then its delivery. Completes DEC-36's last sentence. | - |
| <a id="dec-38"></a>**DEC-38** One OK per phone task; free mode asks only for private data | 2026-11-01 | The owner found an approval for every tap too much ("I want to give it permission to use the phone"). An agent asks once for a whole task (its apps, the exact texts it types, up to an hour); the owner's time window becomes a free mode where typing and opening apps and addresses run without asking unless the text holds an e-mail address, phone number, IBAN or key; a request already waiting goes ahead when the permission comes. Money, passwords, blocked apps and Stop are unchanged. | DEC-23 |
| <a id="dec-39"></a>**DEC-39** SQLite for the swarm | 2026-11-02 | The owner confirmed SQLite for the swarm's Ledger ("keep SQLite for the swarm database ... Yes do all"): one writer (the Swarm service), WAL, a nightly snapshot the restore drill checks, 7 MB. Postgres would add a second failure point and buy nothing on one machine. Closes design decision 2 (SWM-73). | - |
| <a id="dec-45"></a>**DEC-45** The stack is a platform; home is one deployment | 2026-11-08 | The owner asked on 8 Nov 2026 to build what the analysis of a work proposal recommended, so that the stack is the base for work too: "The ai swarm stack shall be a platform to run ai systems like the one we run in my home. My home stuff is an example deployment." and "The windows worker stuff has to be designed well." So: deployments/<name>/ holds what a deployment chose (features, model policy, data class, egress list, constraint values); home switches every feature on and behaves as before. Windows runs only job kinds the owner declared and approved there, as a separate standard account (aiworker), carried by a broker under the owner's account because no other account can read WSL; WSL stays cut off (DEC-43 holds). Customer work is kept in by the network (an egress list), not only by rules in code. A work deployment takes only release tags the owner signed off. Design: docs/PLATFORM.md; approvals at work: docs/WORK-APPROVALS.md. | - |
| <a id="dec-44"></a>**DEC-44** The memory stack | 2026-11-07 | The owner asked for the memory stack to be designed properly with requirements engineering (one memory for every agent, bloat fought, navigable by AI, sectors with their own access, skills from repeated work, maintenance by an agent, coding memory first), agreed the design of docs/MEMORY-STACK.md and answered its decisions on 7 Nov 2026: D1 "completely autonomous": the memory agent acts without his approval (two of his facts that disagree are settled by date, the newer holds; the move list leaves long-term memory; a dispute research could not settle stays marked), every change logged with its undo, while the memory trials pass; D2 "generous budgets. 6k is far too small. We need to experiment to find the right balance": what each run is given is sized by kind and tuned by the share it uses; D3 and D4 (proactive offers within bounds, one memory tool) as designed; and "big bang, then commissioning": built as one change (MEM-22 to MEM-50) and commissioned on the live stack (docs/MEMORY-COMMISSIONING.md). It widens DEC-40, under which contradictions of his own facts stayed his to decide. | DEC-40 |
| <a id="dec-43"></a>**DEC-43** The stack asks Windows; Windows fetches | 2026-11-06 | The owner wanted the stack to talk to Claude Code outside WSL. WSL stays cut off (interop and automount off, no port): a small script on Windows fetches requests from a folder and treats them as data, the way the host probe fetches a disk scan. Status needs no approval; a question goes to Claude with reading tools only, in folders listed on Windows where WSL cannot write; only a turn of the owner's own chat can ask. Changes on Windows would need the owner's tap on the whole job and are not built. Build 27, a second Claude inside WSL, was rejected: it could not reach the Windows sessions. | - |
| <a id="dec-42"></a>**DEC-42** The AI phone stays on Wi-Fi | 2026-11-04 | Asked whether the AI phone will ever run without Wi-Fi, on a SIM only, the owner answered "No, always Wi-Fi". Any Wi-Fi counts, the main phone hotspot included, so ADB over Wi-Fi and Tailscale stay the only way in and the agent app of docs/PHONE-ANYWHERE.md Stage 2 is not built. | - |
| <a id="dec-41"></a>**DEC-41** The AI phone has no single-step approvals | 2026-11-04 | The owner again found approving single actions too much ("I do not want to allow every singel action on AI Phone. I want to allow more macro."). DEC-38 had added one OK per task but left a card per action as the fallback when an agent did not ask for a task. That fallback is gone: taps, swipes, keys, typing and opening apps or addresses run only inside an approved job or free mode, and otherwise are refused with the instruction to ask for the job. Money, settings, deleting, private text in free mode, passwords, blocked apps and Stop are unchanged. | - |
| <a id="dec-40"></a>**DEC-40** The Memory Keeper fixes the memory by rules | 2026-11-02 | The owner asked for one dedicated agent that runs periodically and fixes the memory systems, contradictions and false information included, designed carefully to best practice, and answered D2 ("Yes do all"): the memory agent may retire duplicates, test noise and facts on agents' runs, translate facts into English and date passing states itself, softly and restorably, with each change listed and undoable, while its answer-key trial passes; contradictions of his own facts stay his to decide; world knowledge is corrected only through research with sources (docs/MEMORY.md, the Memory Keeper; MEM-21). | DEC-32 |
| <a id="dec-46"></a>**DEC-46** Everything is a task; the Tasks tab is built around them | 2026-11-08 | The owner asked on 8 Nov 2026 to unify tasks and missions ("How long it runs should not define the term"), to keep six tabs with the tasks inside the agents tab yet built around tasks, to see how tasks and agents connect, and to make the crew's duties tasks too. So: a task is what needs to be done and an agent is who does it; they meet in the steps (one agent per step, many agents per task, many tasks per agent). A task's When (once, repeats, on an event, after another task) is a setting. A task is the owner's or the stack's (trials, probes, gates, the crew's duties and questions, onboarding); the owner's lists show the owner's, the stack's are a filter away. Every mission is a task for good; every crew duty is the stack's repeating task; a chat is a task that lives on Chat; tools are what an agent uses, never agents. The second tab is Tasks with five views: Tasks, Activity, Team, Network, Ideas. A mission's or a duty's page is its task's page. Home counts what is the owner's apart from the stack's ideas (one list, AUT-05, DEC-09 in spirit). Internal names (missions/ in the vault, ops/missions.py, the database) stay. Design: design files of 8 Nov. | - |
| <a id="dec-47"></a>**DEC-47** Ideas become AI change requests | 2026-11-08 | The owner said on 8 Nov 2026 that the ideas are too spammy and asked for one agent that reads all of them, fits them into the larger picture and combines them into fewer, coherent, polished change requests that are implementation ready; those are what the owner sees, on a page of each repository, each naming the ideas it grew from, and each idea the task and run that produced it. Ideas stay visible on their tasks and agents, but not prominently. So: ideas are internal raw material; an Editor (a stack agent, a crew duty) sorts each idea (in a request, held, covered, dropped, with a reason) and writes the requests; programs check what can be checked before a request is shown; a request built is committed with its change. Built in steps: 1 ideas tied to where they came from (ACR-01), 2 the Editor sorting only, 3 the Editor writing, 4 Build it into the change pipeline. After the design the owner said "Do all"; the name (the Editor) and the ceiling (5 proposed requests per repository) are the design's defaults until the owner says otherwise. | - |
| <a id="dec-48"></a>**DEC-48** Onboarding runs as fixed steps with quality gates; a model summarises | 2026-11-08 | Onboarding a 1,325-file Java repository on the work deployment on GPT-5 mini said 8/8 done while its code map had 1,325 placeholder nodes and no edges, and the stack's own log at home said the same of every run ("a structural fallback, not a full semantic analysis"): the Understand Anything skill needs Claude Code's subagent channel, which OpenCode lacks, so an agent was asked to run a pipeline it could not run and took shortcuts, and every step was checked for existence, never for quality. So: the code map runs as fixed steps in the container (the plugin's own scan, batching and tree-sitter structure, then nodes and edges from a program; no model, no agent), summaries come from a model one batch per call, and every step has a quality gate and a record (when it last ran, how long, which model, calls, tokens, its numbers). A hollow result is weak, shown as weak, never done. A repository has a kind: the owner's own work gets an engineer's guide (entry points, module map, data model, how to extend it, build and test, fragile areas, glossary) written from its structure; something to evaluate gets the review. The graph view ranks the repository's own code by the links that hold it together, the source pane says which lines it shows and offers the whole file, and the viewer link works off the tailnet. Fixes belong in the stack (the platform), never as local patches on a deployment. | - |
| <a id="dec-49"></a>**DEC-49** Coding agents get a small card and the brief pushed; everything else onboarding makes stays and is reachable | 2026-11-08 | What the owner asked: how coding agents should work with what onboarding generates, in very large codebases too, keeping the codebase's conventions, and whether it helps at all. The studies say generated overview files pushed into the context do not make agents more correct (ETH Zurich, February 2026: about 3 % worse; a two-agent ablation: no effect on correctness, faster when told about slow tests), selected experience and graph tools for finding code help, and conventions hold when a program checks them; the stack's own exam saw coders use only Bash and never call the memory tool. So: a repository card of at most about 1,500 tokens (commands, rules that are not obvious, fragile areas, one line per part, where to start), made without a model from what onboarding learned, is given first to every coding run, with the owner's pins and strikes; the task brief adds the tests to run first and the closest example for a new file; the file note at first read carries the code map's line, and a wide search hears which folders its hits fall in; after each edit the repository's linter runs on the file; the conventions review reads the card. Nothing made is thrown away: the guide, the wiki, the code map and the facts stay for people and are exported for the coder to grep, never pushed. Update makes the card again and checks the wiki against the changed code. Each layer must prove itself in the replay exam's knowledge arm on a large repository, against an arm without memory, on cases after the commit the knowledge was made at. | - |
| <a id="dec-50"></a>**DEC-50** The AI Stack runs on Temporal, with packs and a canvas; professional tools before our own | 2026-11-09 | What the owner asked: reuse professional tools rather than build from scratch, see how the agents work together like Lego Mindstorms, and let Claude choose the engine by research. So: Temporal (MIT) is the core engine on every deployment, with one generic Python graph runner; templates are graphs in a pack's git, drawn and watched on Workflow Builder's editor (Apache 2.0); packs are Claude Code plugin folders; the worker protocol keeps its trust model, Windows stays pull-only; the tracker through atlassian-python-api and mcp-atlassian; agentgateway for tool grants later. DBOS is not taken because its dashboard is proprietary. The review added: references only in the history, encrypted and kept off the NAS on a customer-class deployment; a run pins its template and a replay test guards the runner; child workflows and continue-as-new keep histories small; the Conductor is removed after 14 days without fallback; Temporal upgrades run in the update path. | - |
| <a id="dec-51"></a>**DEC-51** n8n is the stack's workflow pillar; Temporal and our editor go | 2026-11-10 | What the owner found: the new engine layer was hard to reach, half migrated (9 of about 35 recurring jobs) and rough, and four mechanisms decided when work starts. So: n8n (self-hosted, Sustainable Use License, free for personal and internal business use) becomes the one place that decides when work starts and in what order, with its canvas and run history; the stack's services stay the workers behind one step service; workflows live in git; the owner decides in Needs you; packs, places, trackers and artifacts from DEC-50 stay; Temporal, the Conductor's scheduling, the block editor and the crew's timers are removed once no work runs on them. Stability first: the disk guard and the boot check before any move. | ['DEC-50'] |
| <a id="dec-52"></a>**DEC-52** Temporal is removed; n8n is the one orchestrator | 2026-11-10 | What the owner asked: no technical debt, the repository in a clean state. So, after the runner went back to the Conductor for plans (10 Nov 2026, 10:40) and Temporal stood idle: Temporal's four services, its network, its databases' setup, its upgrade path, its SDK in the runner, the builder and the app, the graph runner, the bridge, the cutover, the codec, the preview, the graph template format, the block editor and its build are removed. What the n8n design keeps from DEC-50 stays, freed from Temporal, in ops/domain: packs (now bringing n8n workflows), places, trackers and the forge, roles, saved files and a run's record. The Conductor keeps its own plan checks and runs plans until their move to n8n (WFL-09). The app's Engine, Runs and Templates screens go in the app's own clean-up. | ['DEC-50'] |
| <a id="dec-53"></a>**DEC-53** The app after n8n: one menu, one place for runs, a Stack page for the stack | 2026-11-10 | What the owner asked: the UX polished and updated, with no trace of technical debt (the target design's section 4). So: the Engine, Runs and Templates screens go, their old links landing on Workflows; packs and trackers are one section of the Stack page; a run is followed on its task's page (Where it runs, its steps, each step's log) and on Workflows, so the task map folds into the task page and Telemetry into the Stack page; the menu is Home, Tasks, Workflows (desk), Memory, Code, Chat, Stack; the Stack page holds health, services, resources, tools, costs and connections, and what is not the stack's moves to where it belongs; the connection state says what is true. Every old address keeps landing somewhere sensible. | - |
| <a id="dec-54"></a>**DEC-54** A draft pull request goes out directly; the step service is the run register | 2026-11-10 | For the first work deployment (the CR of 10 Nov 2026), the owner: "Do it as the CR says directly." So: with FORGE_PUSH a coding change for an item is pushed and opened as a draft pull request at once, with no shadow step; it is the one stated exception to shadow mode for the stack's outside writes (GEN-25), because a draft changes nothing people use until they merge it, and the stack never merges. And the lead's design, accepted by the owner: the step service is the run register (WFL-23), holding the item-to-run map, the event ledger and every step's request, answer, files and times; a run's record is made from it, and n8n gives time and order only, never through its internal API. | - |
